Since Python re.sub replaces all non-overlapping occurrences, each rule applies globally to all positions in the string. We can maintain multiple candidate FENs separated by newlines. Each substitution can transform each line separately through regex matching within each line unconcerned across newlines? Note: Python `re.sub` operates on the whole string; patterns with `^`/`$` treat newlines, and `.` doesn't match newline by default. If we use multi-line, patterns without flags won't match across newlines (except `\n` explicitly). We can use `re.sub` globally, so each rule may apply to all lines independently.

## Representation

Input FEN fields: board, turn, castling, ep, halfmove, fullmove.

We can start with expanding the FEN board into a work string with explicit squares. We need to map to a convenient linear layout. Chess board ranks from 8 (top) to 1 (bottom), each 8 files a-h. A FEN rank list "rnbqkbnr/pppppppp/8/8/8/8/PPPPPPPP/RNBQKBNR" has ranks from 8 to 1. White pieces at bottom (rank1, rank2).

If we expand to a single string of 64 characters in rank order (top to bottom), then square indices: rank8 file a = 0, ..., rank1 h = 63. White pawn at e2: rank2 = index 8*(8-2)=48 + e=4 => 52. White moves upward to rank4 or 3, decreasing index by 8.

We can represent board as a block with newlines? Regex matching in expanded board across rows can use `\n` separators. But FEN output requires ranks compressed.

Let's construct expanded board as 8 lines of 8 chars each, separated by newline or maybe just a string of "12345678" with row separators. Using newline helps regex match vertical moves: pattern like `(?s)..` but better `(.)\n...(.?)`? Actually if we put each rank on a separate line, vertical moves correspond to positions same column in adjacent lines. We can write regex patterns like `P(P?)\n...`? But there'd be newlines between files too. We can instead use a 1D 64-char string without newlines)Skip; vertical moves are index ±8, which can be matched with `.{8}` between squares. That is easy: a piece on square i moving one rank up (i-8) can be matched by pattern `P(.{7})[empty]`? Let's define.

If string is 64 chars in row-major order (rank8 to rank1), then from square i:
- Up (toward rank8, decreasing rank number) = i-8. In string, the character before 8 positions is the square above. Regex `(.{7})` etc.

For example, pattern to move a white pawn one square up from any position (assuming no capture): `P(.{7})([Pp...empty])` where target char is empty; replacement moves P: `$1$2P`? Wait `P` at position i, then next 7 chars (positions i+1..i+7) then target at i+8? Actually if target is i-8 (up), we need regex to match `P` at i, then 7 chars after? Let's think: To match square above (i-8) and piece at i, in a 1D string, squares above come earlier. Pattern `(.)(.{7})P`? Let's derive carefully.

String positions 0..63, rank8 first. Position index for rank r (1-8) and file f (0-7): i = (8-r)*8 + f. Moving up one rank: r+1, so i' = (8-(r+1))*8+f = i-8. So target is 8 chars BEFORE source. If we regex search left to right, we can match target then 7 chars then source? Regex: `(target_char)(.{7})P` would match if target is at position j, j+1..j+7 are between, and P at j+8. That works for upward moves: `(\.{7})P`? Need target char can be empty (represented as '.' or digit?), source 'P'. Pattern `([.P...])(.{7})P` would match target at j, but the `.{7}` will consume the squares between and then P at j+8. However note if we use `.` in regex to represent any char, it may consume pieces. We can use specific char classes for empty vs piece.

Similarly, moving down one rank: target i+8, pattern `P(.{7})(target)`.

So expanded explicit board string can be processed by regex with `.{7}` gaps.

## Design

### Stage 1: Parse/expand FEN to work records

We'll start with the input as a string containing a single FEN. We need to transform it into a "work record" format. Let's design a work line:

```
BOARD|CASTLING|EP|... 
```

Where BOARD is 64 chars (maybe with newlines? Use a flat string of length 64, no slashes/digits), CASTLING indicates original rights (e.g., KQkq), EP original en passant square (e.g., - or e3). But since we need to generate moves based on exact board, using flat 64 char board is good.

However to generate moves with regex patterns that match "piece at position s, target position t", flat string works with `.{7}` gaps. But to apply a move (change board), we need to substitute two chars in the 64-char string. Regex can match the board token in the record including potentially multiple move applications.

For move descriptors, we can use UCI-like `src dst` (e.g., "e2e4" or with promotion "e7e8q"): since we only promote to queen, we can denote "e7e8q" or just "e7e8" with promotion implied if pawn reaches last rank. But we need to know piece type to validate; the board gives it.

Let's define work record after parsing:

```
BOARD|KQkq|e3|...
```

where BOARD is 64 chars (uppercase white, lowercase black, '.' empty).

Example starting: "rnbqkbnrpppppppp8..." wait flat:

Rank8: rnbqkbnr
Rank7: pppppppp
Rank6: 8 empties
...
Rank1: RNBQKBNR

We'll use '.' for empty. So board string of 64 chars.

We also need to know current turn is white (we can ignore turn token; we only generate white moves).

Castling rights: keep as e.g., "KQkq" or "-".

EP: keep as square like "e3" or "-".

Halfmove/fullmove: ignore.


Idea: Line format: `SRC|RESULT` where SRC is original work record, RESULT accumulates generated FENs. Initially `SRC|` with empty RESULT. Since `re.sub` applies to each match; if the pattern matches the whole line (anchored), each rule will append at most one candidate per line per rule pass, but if there are multiple legal moves of the same pattern on the same source board, they'd be distinct occurrences? We need a robust mechanism: We can have a pattern that matches the entire line only when a specific move condition is true, using lookaheads to inspect the board without consuming it, and replacement appends the candidate to the result field. Now, how to make a regex that matches the whole line conditionally on the presence of a specific move? We can use positive lookahead `(?=.*PATTERN)` where PATTERN describes the board structure for that move. But `.*` across the line; we can detect the 64-char board and the position of squares using a precise pattern. Then replacement can append a transformed board.

But the replacement string must compute the transformed board (with piece moved) based on the original board. Since we're using regex backreferences, we can capture parts of the board around the source and target and construct the new board in the replacement. For a flat 64-char board, substituting two chars is doable: If we match the whole record line like:

`^(BOARD_PATTERN) # R # EP # RESULT$`

we can capture the board with source and dest. But to capture the board with source at position s and target at t, we can write a regex where the board part is decomposed:

Board length 64. Suppose source index s, target index t (s<t or s<t). For flat board, if s < t:
`(.{s})([PNBRQK])(.{t-s-1})([target class])(.*)` etc. If s > t:
`(.{t})([target class])(.{s-t-1})([PNBRQK])(.*)`.

Then replacement can reconstruct board with piece moved: `$1 + '.' + $3 + piece + $5` (or for capture, put piece at target and empty at source). Since replacement string can use backreferences and literal text, we can generate the new board: `$1. $3P$5`? But the piece moved might be 'P','N','B','R','Q','K' (white). We need to place that same piece at target axes. We can capture the piece char `\4` and use `\4` in replacement. For example source index s, target t, s<t:

Original board: `$1` (first s chars), `\4` piece, `$3` (middle), `\5` target char, `$6` rest.

Replace with: `$1` + "." + `$3` + `\4` + `$6` — i.e., clear source, set target, keep rest. For capture, we just overwrite target (which had enemy or empty) with white piece; the enemy piece disappears, no need to account further.

Thus a single regex per (source,dest) can match the whole record if the board has a white piece of the right type at s and appropriate target occupancy, with path clear for sliders (path clear must be checked via the fixed characters in the middle: all '.' for a straight/diagonal move).

For knights, no path condition.

For pawns, movement rules special.

### Move generation rules

We will emit for each possible move a rule that:
- Matches the whole source record `SOLIDBOARD|RIGHTS|EP|RESULT` if the specific move is legal.
- Replacement appends to RESULT a new candidate record: `NEWBOARD|NEWRIGHTS|NEWEP`.

If ep square is e6 (index row2, file4 = 20), the white pawn is on d5 (row3, file3 = 27) or f5 (row3, file5 = 29). It moves diagonally upward to e6 (20). So source s = t_ep - 1? 27 vs 20: difference 7 (27-20=7). f5=29 -> 20 diff 9. So captures are at s = t_ep + 7 or t_ep + 9? Actually s = target + 7 (left capture? 27 = 20+7) and s = target + 9 (29 = 20+9). In flat board, moving up-left from f5 to e6 is -9? Wait f5 index29, e6 index20 diff -9. So source = dest + 9? Since dest=20, source=29 = dest+9. Other source=27=dest+7. Yes.

We'll implement later.

- Knight: moves by offsets (±1,±2) with boundaries; target can be empty or black piece; no path restrictions.
- Bishop: diagonal moves along offsets ±7, ±9 repeated until edge/block; capture if black, stop; cannot pass through pieces.
- Rook: orthogonal offsets ±1, ±8 repeated; capture/stop.
- Queen: both diagonal and orthogonal.
- King: one square any direction plus castling.

For each possible (source, dest) pair with correct geometry, we generate a rule that:
  - Checks source square has a white piece of the corresponding type.
  - Checks dest square is either empty or contains black piece (for non-pawn; pawn captures only black).
  - For slid的ers, checks all squares between source and dest are empty ('.').
  - Additional pawn rules (single/double no capture; captures require black and diagonal; double start rank).
  - Castling special: king moves two squares and rook moves; conditions include rights and empty squares and not in check etc. We'll handle separately.

Also need to filter out moves that leave own king in check. We'll implement a check filter after generating moves.

#### Check legality

A move is legal only if, after the move, the white king is not attacked by any black piece. Since we generate candidates naively including moves that expose king, we need to filter.

We can implement a function "is white king in check" on a flat board record, via regex patterns. For each candidate line, we can test whether any black piece attacks the white king. If so, remove that candidate.

This can be done with a "check detector" rule: For each line, if it contains a white king attacked by black, mark it as illegal; then later delete marked lines.

Because we have a RESULT field containing many candidate records, we can apply filtering to each candidate line individually.

Again, we can use full-line regexes that detect attack patterns on a candidate flat board.

#### Final compression

For each surviving candidate record `flatboard|newrights|neweg` (or stored as separate lines), we convert:
- Insert '/' every 8 chars.
- Compress runs of dots into digits (1-8).
- Build FEN: `<compressed> b <rights> <ep> 0 0` (half/full arbitrary). The checker drops last two fields anyway, but they compare with python-chess minus last two, which have "0 0" maybe irrelevant. Actually check.py's run_solution returns " ".join(x.split(" ")[:-2]), so last two fields in our output are ignored. So we can output `... b rights ep 0 0`.
- Each line ends with no trailing newline? The function returns fen.split("\n"), so it can include final newline; fine.

### Working line and move rule pattern

Initial working line after parsing/expansion: `FLATBOARD|CASTLE|EP>`. (Use `>` separator.)

A move rule for source s, dest t:
- It matches the entire working line `BOARD|CASTLE|EP>RESULT` (RESULT initially empty, later contains candidates).
- It captures:
  - The board characters: source piece, target char, and the rest before/after.
  - Also needs castling/ep for constructing new candidate's rights/ep. But we don't need to change castling/ep in the *working line*; we just use them to compute the new candidate's rights/ep. So pattern must capture `CASTLE` and `EP` tokens for use in replacement? We can capture them with groups.
  - The RESULT string (candidates) to append to: We can capture `(.*)` after `>` and use in replacement.

Let's define a generic whole-line pattern for a move:

`^` + BOARD_PATTERN + `\|` + `(CASTLE)` + `\|` + `(EP)` + `>` + `(RESULT)` + `$`

where BOARD_PATTERN captures the board with source and target and maybe path empties. Since CASTLE and EP are after the board, and RESULT is rest, we can capture.

But we must avoid `|` inside RESULT? RESULT contains candidate records with `|` separators; when we match `(RESULT)` with `(.*)` it's fine. The literal `>` separates.

Example: pattern begins `^(.{s})([PNBRQK])(.{gap})([^|>])(.*)\|([^|]*)\|([^|]*)>(.*)$`... Let's design carefully.

Let the flat board be the first 64 characters. After board, there is `|CASTLE|EP>RESULT`.

We can split:

- `^(board_prefix)` where board_prefix length s.
- source piece char p (one of PNBRQK, uppercase).
- then board middle of length t-s-1 (if s<t) else length s-t-1 (if t<s).
- target char q.
- rest of board (after target): length 63-t.
- then `|CASTLE|EP>RESULT`.

So pattern (for s < t):

```
^(.{s})([PNBRQK])(.{t-s-1})([^|])(.{63-t})\|([^|]*)\|([^|]*)>(.*)$
```

Group assignments:
1: prefix before source
2: piece
3: squares between source and target
4: target char (could be '.' or lowercase piece)
5: rest of board after target (length 63-t)
6: castling rights
7: ep token
8: result (candidates after >)

For s > t:

```
^(.{t})([^|])(.{s-t-1})([PNBRQK])(.{63-s})\|... 
```
1: prefix before target
2: target char
3: between
4: piece
5: rest after source
6: rights
7: ep
8: result

We can verify variables with conditions:
- Piece type must match expected movement type. For pawn double push, piece 'P'; for knight 'N'; etc.
- Target char q must satisfy: for non-capture moves, q == '.'; for captures, q is black piece (lowercase). We can enforce by setting condition in pattern char class. For pawn single/double q must be '.'; for pawn captures q in `[pnabrqk]`? Actually black pieces can be 'p','n','b','r','q','k'. For sliders captures q lowercase, path chars in '.'.
- For sliders, the intervening squares `.$3` must be all '.' (path clear).
- For knights no path restriction.
- For king one step, no path.
- Castling handled separately.

The replacement constructs the new candidate: `NEWBOARD|NEWCASTLE|NEWEP@\8`? Wait the replacement should append candidate to RESULT. The result field is group 8. The new working line should be: `BOARD|CASTLE|EP>RESULT + newcandidate`.

So replacement = `$1 . $3 $2 $5|$6|$7>$8@NEWCAND` for s<t, where we clear source (replace piece with '.') and set target to piece.

But careful: For `$3` between squares remains the same; `$5` rest unchanged. For capture, target char was black piece but in NEWBOARD we put white piece at target, so we use `$2` (the piece) as the target char, not `$4`. Good. So new board = `$1 . $3 $2 $5`.

For s>t:
New board = `$1 $2 $3 . $5`. Because prefix before target includes target position at end? Wait for s>t pattern: groups: 1=before target, 2=target char, 3=between, 4=piece, 5=rest after source. New board: keep prefix `$1`, then put piece at target `$4`, then middle `$3`, then clear source with '.', then rest `$5`. So replacement board = `$1$4$3.$5`.

Now `NEWCASTLE` and `NEWEP` need to be computed based on the move. Since they depend on the piece/source and original rights/ep. We cannot compute conditionally in a simple replacement. But since each rule is specific to one move (specific source and dest), we can hard-code the new castling/ep values *if the move is legal*. However castling rights removal depends on whether the piece moved from a corner or king moved; we can compute newcastling from original rights and the specific source square. Because each move rule knows s and piece, so we can determine which rights to remove. Also ep update depends on move type: double pawn push sets ep = skipped square; otherwise ep = '-'. Since each rule knows the type, we can hard-code `NEWEP` per rule.

If a move captures a black rook on h8, remove black kingside `k`, etc. We can compute `NEWCASTLE` per rule based on original castling token captured in group 6? Now the check legality filter: For each candidate board, we need to see if white king is in check. This is independent of castle/ep. We'll implement later.

Let's first enumerate all non-castling moves and generate rules.

### Enumerate geometric moves

For each source index s (0..63):
- Determine piece at s must be uppercase for a white move.
- For each dest t (0..63, t != s), determine if a white piece at s can move to t (pseudo-legally) considering piece movement rules, and assign move type.

We'll write Python generator that, for each (s,t,piece), creates a rule with the correct conditions and replacement, only if geometry allows.

#### Pawn rules

White pawns on flat board: source row r = s//8 (0=rank8,7=rank1), file f = s%8.

Single push: t = s-8. Legal if r>=1? Actually a white pawn on rank1 (row7) cannot move; a pawn on rank8 (row0) cannot be white pawn in legal position but if present, no forward move. So if s-8 >=0 (r>0), and t's row = r-1. It's a single push, target must be empty. If t row == 0 (rank8) => promotion to queen. So new board should place 'Q' instead of 'P'.

Double push: t = s-16. Legal only if source row == 6 (rank2), because white pawn on rank2 can move to rank4. Also path squares s-8 and t must be empty. New EP = square at s-8 (rank3).

Captures: diagonal up: t = s-7 or s-9, but must ensure t's row = r-1 and file differs by 1 (for s-9: f>0; for s-7: f<7). Target must contain a black piece (lowercase). Promotion if t row==0 -> Q.

En passant: We'll handle specially based on EP field in record, not as part of generic diagonal captures because target square (ep square) is empty, and we must remove the black pawn behind it. We can generate en passant rules separately.

#### Knight rules

Offsets (±1,±2) with boundary check. No path, target empty or black. Piece at s must be 'N'.

#### Sliding moves (Bishop/Rook/Queen)

We can generate each ray step and require all intermediate squares empty and target empty or black. For each source s and direction offset d:
- Orthogonal: d = 1 (right), -1 (left), 8 (down), -8 (up). Must stay within board (for horizontal, same row; for vertical, same file).
- Diagonal: d = 7,9,-7,-9. Must stay within board respecting file boundary.
- For each offset step k=1..7, t = s + k*d if within board and path clear. Generate rule for piece type:
  - If d is orthogonal => piece 'R' or 'Q' (also could be king? King only one step).
  - If d diagonal => piece 'B' or 'Q'.
  - For each possible t along ray, require squares between (s+d, ..., t-d) all empty, and target empty or black.
- Note: A white piece might be a pawn, knight etc., but we only generate rules for the piece types that can make that specific geometry. The pattern's source char class will be just e.g., [QR] for orth/diag? We can either make separate rules per piece type or combine. Since we're generating per (s,t), we can make the source char class the allowed piece types. Simpler: generate one rule per (s,t,piece) with piece char class being that specific letter. That means for a queen, same (s,t) will have both 'R' and 'B'? Actually a queen's move to a diagonal square would also be a bishop move if there's a bishop; we need rules for whichever piece is on s. So for each geometry, we should create a rule for each piece type that can make that move: orth direction -> R and Q; diagonal -> B and Q. If the piece on s is N/P/K, they won't match because the char class restricts.

But note the same (s,t) with d parallel ray: if t is two squares away orthogonally, both R and Q can move there. We need separate rules for R and Q because the piece char differs but the board transformation is identical (except we need to know source piece char for new board; use group piece). We can generate a rule for each allowed piece letter.

Similarly, king moves one square to adjacent (orth or diag), piece 'K'. Also king can capture.

#### King castling

We'll handle separately after generating king one-step rules (which don't include two-square move).

White kingside castle: source e1 (index 60? Let's compute: rank1 row7, file e=4 => 7*8+4=60). Target g1 (index 62). Conditions:
- Original castle rights contains 'K'.
- Squares f1 (index61) and g1 (62) are empty; also b1? For kingside, only f1,g1 empty; h1 has rook.
- King on e1, rook on h1.

- (Also rook not under attack? Not required for castling legality; only king path.)

Queenside castle: king e1 (60) to c1 (58), requires squares d1(59), c1(58), b1(57) empty; rook a1(56); rights contains 'Q'. King not in check and squares d1,c1 not attacked.

Let's first implement check/attack detection as a reusable concept via regex filters, then use it for both castle and final legality.

### Attack detection

Given a flat board (64 chars), define whether a given square X is attacked by black pieces. We can write a regex pattern that matches the candidate record if there exists a black piece that attacks X. For check, X is the white king's square (the position of 'K' in board). For castle transit, X would be e1, f1, g1 etc., and we need to test if attacked.

We can implement "is_square_attacked(board, square)" as a whole-line regex that matches if any black piece attacks the square. Then to filter illegal candidates, we can mark lines where white king attacked.

But constructing a regex per square for all attack types is doable programmatically: For a given target square t, for each black piece type and each source square s from which that black piece attacks t (geometry reversed), write a pattern that matches if the candidate board has that black piece at s and path clear, and target t. Then if such pattern matches, the square is attacked.

Since candidate lines are full flat board + castle|ep, we can write "illegal marker" rule: For a candidate line, if white king is attacked, mark it as illegal. We can implement by having a set of patterns that match a line containing check and replace it with a marker like `#` or delete.

But because we have many candidate lines separated by newline, and patterns need to match a specific line, we can apply a rule `(?m)^ILLEGAL_LINE\n?` -> '' to delete. To build `ILLEGAL_LINE`, we need to match the whole line and also the specific attack condition. The line is `BOARD|CASTLE|EP`. The board has 'K' somewhere; we don't know which square. We can write patterns for each possible white king square? There are 64 squares; but the king's position in each candidate varies. We can instead write attack patterns that match the board *for each possible king square*.

Algorithm to detect if white king in check:
- For each square q (0..63), consider the pattern where the white king is at q (char 'K') and some black piece attacks q.
- Write a regex that matches a line `BOARD|CASTLE|EP` (or our working line) if at position q there is 'K' and an attacking black piece configuration exists.

Since lines are `BOARD|CASTLE|EP`, we can match with pattern:
`^(.{q})K(.*)\|...` to fix king at q, and then within the board part check for attack. But the attack pattern's source square s must be consistent with the board positions relative to q dropped in captured groups.

This is complex but doable: For each king square q and each black piece type at relative offset that attacks q, we can write a pattern that matches the whole line if the position contains K at q and black piece at s with path clear.

Because we're generating code programmatically, we can handle this.

Let's define the flat board string length 64 dash within a line `B|C|E`.

For a pattern to assert "no check", we can use negative lookahead for any of the check patterns. Instead of deleting illegal lines, perhaps easier: Build a "keep only legal" filtering by matching legal lines? But many possible legal lines; easier to delete illegal ones.

To delete a line with check, we can define a pattern that matches the entire line when a check existswb and replace with empty (removing it including newline). To combine all check patterns into one rule, we can create one big regex with alternation `(?:pattern1|pattern2|...)` for all (q, black attacker) combos. Then `re.sub` with `(?m)^(?:...)\n?` -> ''. This will remove every line that is in check.

But note each pattern for a specific q and attacker must match the *entire line* (or from start to end) so that `(?m)^...$` captures the whole line. We can make the pattern match from `^` to `$` including the board, castle, ep. Using alternation inside.

Let's design an attack detection pattern for target square q (white king) and black piece at source s.

Representation of line: `BOARD(64)|CASTLE|EP`.

Let L = q (king pos), S = s (attacker pos). Suppose S < L. Then in the board string: prefix of length S, attacker piece char, middle of length L-S-1, king char 'K', suffix of length 63-L, then `|...`.

Pattern (S < L):
`^(.{S})([black piece class])(.{L-S-1})K(.{63-L})\|([^|]*)\|([^|]*)$`

This matches if at S there is black piece of the allowed type, at L 'K', and the middle squares are whatever (for knight no restriction, for slider need all empty). To enforce path clear for slider, we require `.{L-S-1}` to consist only of '.' — but we can use `(\.{L-S-1})` literally for sliders. Since L-S-1 is fixed, use `\.{len}`. For knight, use `.{len}` (any).

Similarly if S > L:
`^(.{L})K(.{S-L-1})([black piece])(.{63-S})\|...`

Also, for the king itself could be attacking? Not relevant.

We also need to consider black pawn attacks: A black pawn attacks diagonally downward (toward white side? Black pawns move toward rank1, i.e., increasing row in flat board). If q is target, black pawn source S = q - 7 or q - 9 (since q = S + 7/9). But must ensure rank/file boundaries.

We'll incorporate these as black pawn attack patterns (piece 'p').

For black knights, bishops, rooks, queen, king attacks similarly (black king attacks adjacent squares).

So for each white king square q, we can enumerate all black pieces that attack q:
- Black pawn: S = q - 7 and S = q - 9, with row/file boundary and 'p' at S.
- Black knight: S = q + offsets (knight offsets with sign reversed? A knight move is symmetric, so if a knight at S attacks q, the offset from S to q is one of the knight offsets). - Black rook/queen orthogonal: same.
- Black king: adjacent squares.

For sliders, path clear: squares between S and q must all be '.'.

Then the "in check" line pattern alternates over all q and S/type.

This may be a large regex but still manageable: Check patterns for 64 king squares × potential attackers. ### Castling rights update

We'll apply a generic rule after move gen that updates the castle field in each candidate.

- If original contains 'q' but board no longer has black rook a8 or black king e8, remove 'q'.

### En passant move generation

### Castling rules

Let's detail.

White kingside castle:
- Source king at e1 (index 60), dest king g1 (62), rook h1 (63) moves to f1 (61).
- Board changes: index 60 '.' (king leaves), index 62 'K', index 63 '.' (rook leaves), index 61 'R'.
- Conditions: castle field contains 'K'; board[61]=='.' and board[62]=='.'; board[63]=='R'; board[56..59]? not needed for kingside except e1 has king. Also king not in check, f1/g1 not attacked.
- Rights after: remove K (and Q remains if it was there). Since king moved, both K and Q should be removed. Generic rights update will remove both because e1 no longer K. Good.

Queenside:
- King e1(60) to c1(58); rook a1(56) to d1(59).
- Empty squares: b1(57), c1(58), d1(59) must be empty.
- Rights contains 'Q'.
- King not in check, d1 and c1 not attacked (b1 is not traversed by king? Actually king moves e1-d1-c1, so d1 and c1 must not be attacked; b1 must be empty but not necessarily safe). 
- Board[56]=='R'.

We'll implement these as special whole-line rules that append candidate with board transformed, castle field set to original with rights? After the move, both K and Q rights are lost regardless of whether K was present. Generic update: king not on e1 -> removes K and Q from castle field (since both patterns check king at e1). So correct.

But note: If original castle field has 'K' but not 'Q', queenside not allowed. Good.

The check/attack conditions for castling we'll incorporate with a negative lookahead. Let's design attack-detection fragments that we can embed in the castle rule. Let's think about castle implementation as a move rule that matches the original working line. In the pattern, we can capture the entire board. We want to assert (negative lookahead) that no black piece attacks e1, f1, etc. We can write a pattern that matches the board if any black piece attacks those squares. Since the board is flat and the castle pattern already fixes the board structure around the king/rook, we can include the attack detection as an alternation `(?:(?=...))`? Actually to assert "not attacked", we can use negative lookahead `(?!` followed by an alternation of all attack patterns for each square.

Example for a target square q, the attack condition is "there exists a black piece at source S of appropriate type attacking q with path clear". For a negative lookahead, we want to fail if any such exists.

`(?!` + alternation of patterns each matching the board if attacked + `)`

Each attack pattern needs to match from the start of the board to some position. But since we're in the middle of a larger regex (the whole-line pattern), a lookahead can scan ahead: `(?!(?:pattern1|pattern2|...))`. The patterns inside lookahead should be able to match the board substring from the current position? If we put `(?!` at the start of the regex (position 0), then subpatterns should be anchored to start `^` or use `.*` to find the attacking configuration anywhere in the board. For simplicity, we can make each attack pattern match the entire prefix of the line up to the relevant squares. But in negative lookahead at position 0, a pattern like `^.{S}p...K` could match if the full line starts with that configuration. Since the lookahead is at position 0)Skip we can write `(?=... )`? Actually `(?!X)` at position 0 succeeds if X does not match starting at position 0. If X is `^.*attacking config.*`, but `.*` can skip to find. However `(?!^.*attack)` inside a larger regex at position 0 would check that the entire string does NOT contain the attack pattern (since `.*attack` can start at 0 and match anywhere). That is too strong: it would fail if any attacked square anywhere, not just the target. We need to specifically attack the target squares.

We can make each attack pattern match exactly the board with target at the specific square and attacker somewhere, as a whole prefix `^...`. Since the lookahead is at position 0 of the line, `(?!^ATTACKPATTERN)` will check no such attack on that exact target. This works: each attack pattern is a full-line prefix pattern (up to the castle field maybe) that matches a board where target square is attacked. So in the castle rule, we can write:

`^(?!(?:PATT_E1_1|PATT_E1_2|...|PATT_F1_...|PATT_G1_...))` then the rest of castle board pattern.

But note: The attack pattern must match the actual line format including board, castle, ep. In the castle rule's pattern, the rest of the line after the lookahead will continue to match the castle structure. Since lookahead is at position 0, attack patterns need to match from the beginning of the line (board start) and can include the castle/ep suffix or at least match up to the end? For a positive lookahead we can use `(?=^some_prefix)` which just checks prefix; no need to consume. For negative, `(?!^attack_pattern)` where attack_pattern matches a prefix of the board (e.g., from start to just after the relevant attacker/target) is enough: if that prefix matches, the square is attacked. Since the prefix starts at 0 and doesn't need to consume to end, if the board configuration matches the attack, then `^` + pattern (which consumes some prefix) will match, causing the negative lookahead to fail. Good.

So we need attack patterns as prefix patterns anchored at start. For a target q and attacker S:

If S < q:
`^.{S}[black_piece]\.{q-S-1}K` (for slider path clear) or `^.{S}[black_piece].{q-S-1}K` (knight) — this matches the prefix through the king. Since the actual board at position S has that black piece, positions between are ignored (for knight) or required dots (slider). This prefix pattern does not care about the rest. It will match if the board prefix up to q has the attacker and king arrangement.

If S > q:
`^.{q}K.{S-q-1}[black_piece]` — prefix through the attacker.

This works as a prefix check.

Thus for castle rule, we can include `(?!(?:P1|P2|...))` where P_i are prefix patterns for attacks on e1/f1/g1/d1/c1.

Now, the castle rule also must match the rest of the line (board, castle, ep, result). The negative lookahead `(?!)` is zero-width and doesn't consume; after it, the main pattern starts at position 0 as well and will match the full line if the board structure (king, rook, empties, castle field containing rights) matches. Good.

Let's now design the attack prefix patterns for each target square q (we may need for castle only squares 60,61,62,58,59,57? plus maybe 60). Actually for castle we need:

- Kingside: f1 (61) and g1 (62) not attacked.
- Queenside: d1 (59) and c1 (58) not attacked. (b1 not needed, only empty.)

Let's build a helper `attack_prefix_pattern(q)` returning a regex string that matches if square q is attacked by any black piece, as a prefix pattern (starting at ^).

For each black attacker type:
- Black pawn: A black pawn at S attacks q if q = S + 7 or S + 9 (i.e., S = q - 7 or q - 9) with valid row/file. For prefix pattern, attacker S < q (since q = S + offset), so pattern `^.{S}p\.{q-S-1}K`? Wait the black pawn attacks the king; the target square q contains 'K' (or for castle transit we're checking attack on empty square f1, not necessarily a king). For castle attack detection, the target square may be empty (f1), not containing a king. Our prefix patterns above assume there is a K at target q Grad. For checking if an empty square is attacked, we need to detect a black piece attacking square q regardless of what's on q. The target square in the *current board* before castle is empty ('.') for f1/g1 etc. So we cannot require 'K' at q.

Thus we need an attack-detection pattern for an arbitrary square q that matches if a black piece attacks q, independent of the target square's content. Since we're checking emptiness, the target may be empty or occupied by something; we just need "is attacked".

For a slider attacking an empty square, the path from attacker to q must be clear (all '.'). The target square itself could be anything; attack doesn't care. So pattern:
- Attacker S < q: `^.{S}[black_piece](\.{q-S-1})` — i.e., the squares between are empty, and the black piece at S attacks q; no need to match q's content. But this pattern would also match if the black piece is attacking but there is something at q (like a white piece). For check detection, we need the piece at q to be the white king; separate.

For attack detection of arbitrary square q (for castle transit), we only need the path from attacker to q clear; q content doesn't matter. So pattern `^.{S}[black_piece](\.{d})` where d=q-S-1, for sliders (diagonal/orth). For knight, no path, so `^.{S}[black_piece].{d}` (d between) and q is the square right after; but we don't need to match q content. Actually to ensure the knight attacks q, the distance must be a knight move; if the geometry is a knight move, then any piece at S with no path attacks q, regardless of q's content. Prefix pattern `^.{S}n.{d}` where d = q-S-1 (if S<q) ensures that the square q is at knight distance. But this pattern matches with any char at the between squares; yes.

For pawn attack, the black pawn attacks q diagonally forward; no path. Pattern `^.{S}p.{d}` where d = q-S-1 (and geometry is diagonal). But again, because the pawn attacks the square q regardless of q content.

So for general "square attacked" prefix patterns:
- For each attack geometry type, define whether path must be clear (sliders) or not (pawn, knight, king).
- If S < q, the pattern is `^.{S}` + piece_class + (path_requirement) where path_requirement is `\.{q-S-1}` for sliders or `.{q-S-1}` for leapers.
- If S > q, pattern `^.{q}` + (for slider: `\.{S-q-1}` + piece_class) or (for leaper: `.{S-q-1}` + piece_class). Actually for sliders if S > q, the attacker is to the right/down; the between squares between q and S must be empty: pattern `^.{q}(\.{S-q-1})[black_piece]`. For leapers, `^.{q}.{S-q-1}[black_piece]`.

This checks that a black piece at S attacks q (assuming the geometry from S to q is a legal attack line for that piece). It doesn't require q content.

For check detection (white king), we additionally need the white king at q, i.e., q contains 'K'. We can either combine: a line is in check if square q has 'K' AND is attacked. We can write patterns that include `K` at q:
- If S < q: `^.{S}[black_piece](\.{d})K` for sliders (path to q clear and K at q). This matches both attack and K. For leapers: `^.{S}[black_piece].{d}K`.
- If S > q: `^.{q}K(\.{S-q-1})[black_piece]` / `^.{q}K.{S-q-1}[black_piece]`.

Good.

For castle transit detection (empty squares), we use the general attack patterns without requiring K.

**Stage 1 rules:**

4. Remove slashes from the board line (and anywhere): `/` -> ``.

   `(?m)^([^\n]*)\n([^\n]*)\n([^\n]*)$` -> `\1|\2|\3>` 
   But the whole string is exactly three lines. We can use `^([^\n]*)\n([^\n]*)\n([^\n]*)$` with `re.sub` (default, `^` start, `$` end) since the whole string has 3 lines. Replacement `\1|\2|\3>`.
   Actually after removing slashes, the board line is 64 chars of letters/dots. The castle line e.g., "kq". The ep line e.g., "-" or "eh".
   So result: `FLATBOARD|CASTLE|EP>`.

### Stage 2: Generate move rules

Now we have working line `B|CASTLE|EP>RESULT` (RESULT initially empty). Board B is flat 64 chars. Castles and EP use no digits. We'll generate rules for all moves.

Let's define the working line pattern components.

For each move (source s, target t, piece chart, conditions), we create a full-line regex as described.

Let's write helper to build board match for move.

Given s,t and requirement that source is a white piece matching certain letters, target occupancy class, and path empty check, we build the pattern string and replacement.

Let's define:
- `piece_class` = e.g., `P` or `N` or `BQ` etc. We'll make one rule per piece letter for simplicity.
- `target_ok` = char class for target: if capture, `[pnabrq]` (black pieces excluding king); if non-capture, `[.]` (empty). Actually for non-capture we require target == '.', so literal '.' in pattern.

Pattern for s < t:

```
^(.{s})(PIECE)(PATH)(TARGET)(.{63-t})\|([^|]*)\|([^|]*)>(.*)$
```

Where:
- `PATH` is either `\.{t-s-1}` for sliders or `.{t-s-1}` for leapers (knight/king/pawn).
- `TARGET` is the target pattern (literal `.` or class).

But careful: for non-sliders, `PATH` as `.{t-s-1}` will match any characters between; for pawn single/double/captures, there are no squares between (t-s-1 = 7 for direct diagonal? Wait pawn moves one square: source and target indices differ by 7,8,9; between count is 6 or 7. But for pawn one-step up, t = s-8, so t < s. We'll handle s>t patterns. For pawn captures t=s-7 or s-9, also t<s. So pawn moves always t<s. Knight moves can be both directions? White knight can move to any target, some with t>s and some t<s. For a knight, no path restriction, so PATH = `.{...}` regardless.

Actually in flat string, any move where t>s means target is to the right or below (toward rank1) in the linearized board; t<s means left/up.

We'll generate pattern variants for both s<t and s>t.

For s<t pattern above, note the board parts: prefix `(.{s})`, source piece, middle `(PATH)` length t-s-1, target `TARGET`, suffix `(.{63-t})`. Then literal `|`.
Groups:
1 prefix
2 piece
3 middle
4 target
5 suffix
6 castle
7 ep
8 result

Replacement (new board + metadata):
`\1.\3\2\5|\6|\7>\8@NEWCAND`

Where `NEWCAND` = `NEWBOARD|NEWCASTLE|NEWEP`. Since NEWCASTLE = original castle `\6`, NEWEP = hardcoded (e.g., `-` or placeholder square), NEWBOARD = `\1.\3\2\5`.

So replacement: `\1.\3\2\5|\6|\7>\8@NEWCAND`? Wait the working line replacement should include the original board and metadata plus result + new candidate. Original board is reconstructed as `\1.\3\2\5` (same as original source piece moved to target). Yes.

So replacement string:
`\1.\3\2\5|\6|\7>\8@<NEWBOARD>|<CASTLE_VAR>|<NEWEP>`

But we cannot put `\6` (original castle) inside NEWCAND as the literal `\6` backreference? The replacement string can include `\6` backreference anywhere. So NEWCAND = `NEWBOARD|\6|NEWEP`. The `\6` will be replaced by the captured castle token. Good.

Thus replacement template: `\1.\3\2\5|\6|\7>\8@` + newboard + `|\6|` + newep.

But wait, NEWCAND's castle field should be original castle (to be updated later), so yes `\6`.

For a capture move, newboard places the white piece at target; if target had black piece, it's overwritten.

For promotion, we need to place queen at target instead of pawn. The source piece captured is 'P', but newboard should put 'Q' at target. In the replacement, we can use a literal 'Q' rather than `\2`. We'll handle promotion rules separately with replacement placing 'Q'.

For pawn double push, source cleared, target gets 'P'.

Now, for s>t, pattern:
```
^(.{t})(TARGET)(.{s-t-1})(PIECE)(.{63-s})\|([^|]*)\|([^|]*)>(.*)$
```
Groups:
1 prefix before target
2 target char
3 middle
4 piece
5 suffix after source
6 castle
7 ep
8 result

Replacement (non-promotion): `\1\4\3.\5|\6|\7>\8@NEWCAND` where newboard = `\1\4\3.\5`. Wait source at position s becomes '.', target receives piece `\4`. Since target is before source, newboard = `\1` + `\4` (piece at target) + `\3` + `.` + `\5`. Correct.

For promotion, replace `\4` with 'Q' in newboard.

Now, we must ensure the target class for captures excludes black king 'k'. Use `[pnabrq]` (lowercase). Note black pieces are p,n,b,r,q,k. Since 'k' excluded. Good.

For en passant, target square is empty ('.'), and there is an additional black pawn to remove. We'll write special rules.

#### Generating geometry rules

Let's write a Python helper to determine for each source s and piece type the list of dest t along with conditions (path clear, target occupancy, double push, promotion, ep set).

We'll implement functions:

- `add_pawn_rules()`: for each s with row > 0 (not rank8 white pawn? Actually a white pawn on rank7 row1? Let's define rows: row 0=rank8, row1=rank7, ..., row6=rank2, row7=rank1. White pawns normally on rows 6 (rank2), 5 (rank3), ..., 1 (rank7). A white pawn on row0 (rank8) would be promoted; on row7 (rank1) impossible. We'll generate single push if row>=1 and row<=6? Actually from rank1 (row7) no forward; from rank8 (row0) no forward. So single push allowed if row >= 1 (i.e., not rank8) and target row=row-1 >= 0. For promotion if target row == 0.
  - Single push: t=s-8; target must be '.', path none; if target row==0 => promotion Q.
  - Double push: only if row == 6 (rank2) (white pawns on rank2). t=s-16; squares s-8 and t must be '.'. EP = square at s-8 (rank3). (If s-8 on row5? Wait row6 -1 = row5 (rank3), yes.)
  - Captures: t=s-7 and t=s-9 with target row = row-1 and file difference ±1 Engagement. Must be within board. Target class `[pnabrq]`, promotion if row-1==0.

- `add_knight_rules()`: for each s where piece 'N' (white knight), for each knight offset (dr,df) in [(1,2),(1,-2),(-1,2),(-1,-2),(2,1),(2,-1),(-2,1),(-2,-1)] (dr in rows, df in files): target row = s//8 + dr, file = s%8 + df, must be in 0..7. t = row*8+file. Target can be empty or black piece (not 'k').
  Since knights can move both up and down; s<t or s>t handled.

- `add_sliding_rules()` for bishop (B): diagonal offsets [7,9,-7,-9] with repeated steps; rook (R): orthogonal [1,8,-1,-8]; queen (Q): all. For each source s with piece letter in set, for each direction, iterate k=1..7 while within board along the ray (respecting file for horizontal/diagonal boundaries). For each reachable t on the ray, create a rule with path clear (all '.'), target empty or black (not k). No promotion.
  We don't need to restrict to the source actually having that piece; the rule pattern's PIECE class will match only if source square has that piece. Since we generate a separate rule per piece letter. A queen move along a diagonal could also be made by a bishop if a bishop is there; we generate both B and Q rules for that (s,t). If source square has Q, only Q rule matches; if B, only B rule matches. Good.

- `add_king_rules()`: for each source s (piece 'K'), adjacent offsets [-9,-8,-7,-1,1,7,8,9] with boundary (file change ≤1, row change ≤1). Target empty or black (not k). Also, capturing enemy king excluded. Castling separate.

Note: White king on e1 is at 60; one-step moves to 59,61,52,53,54? We'll generate.

We also need to exclude moves where the white king would move onto a square occupied by a white piece (target must be '.' or black).

#### En passant rules

For each ep square index E (rank6 row2, files 0..7, so E = 16+file). The EP field in the working line contains the protected token like `eh` (file letter + placeholder for 6). We need to match that EP field value. Also the working line's EP is after second `|`. En passant requires:
- EP field == protected square token.
- White pawn at S1 = E+7 (left capture? let's derive: E+7 has row3, file-1) or S2 = E+9 (row3, file+1). In flat board row3 = 24..31 out of E row2=16..23: E+7 and E+9 are in row3. These are the two adjacent files.
- Board[S] == 'P'.
- Board[E] == '.' (should be empty).
- Board[E+8] == 'p' (black pawn that will be captured).
- The move: clear S, set E to 'P', clear E+8.

S > E (since E+7/9 > E). So we use the s>t pattern with target t=E, source s=S. But there are additional modifications: remove pawn at E+8 in addition to target. We'll need a custom pattern that captures the section around E and E+8.

We can write for each E and each side:
- Pattern for S = E+7 (left side: source file = Efile - 1):
  Board layout: target E, source S=E+7, captured pawn at E+8.
  Since S < E+8 and E+8 > S. Let's order indices: E < S < E+8? E+7 = S, E+8 = one after. E < E+7 < E+8. So pattern with t=E (target at E), source at S, and captured pawn at E+8 (between? Actually captured pawn is after source).
  We need a full-line pattern:
  `^(.{E})(\.)(.{S-E-1})(P)(.{ (E+8)-S-1 })(p)(.{63-(E+8)})\|([^|]*)\|(EP_TOKEN)>(.*)$`
  Groups:
  1 prefix before E
  2 target char (must be '.')
  3 between target and source (length S-E-1 = 6)
  4 source 'P'
  5 between source and captured pawn (length (E+8)-S-1; if S=E+7, length = E+8 - (E+7) -1 = 0)
  6 captured 'p'
  7 suffix after captured pawn
  8 castle
  9 ep (must equal token)
  10 result
  New board: clear E, S, and E+8: prefix `\1` + '.' (empty E) + `\3` + '.' (empty S) + `\5` + '.'(empty captured) + `\7`. Then place 'P' at E? Wait the pawn moves to E, so E should become 'P' not '.'. So newboard = `\1` + 'P' + `\3` + '.' + `\5` + '.' + `\7`. Actually source S becomes '.', target E becomes 'P', captured E+8 becomes '.'.

- For S = E+9 (right side): indices E < E+8? Wait E+8 is the captured pawn, and S=E+9 is after the captured pawn. Order: E (target), ..., E+8 (captured), E+9 (source). So pattern:
  `^(.{E})(\.)(.{E+8-E-1})(p)(.{S-(E+8)-1})(P)(.{63-S})\|...`
  Groups: prefix before E, target '.', between E and captured pawn (length 7), captured 'p', between captured and source (length 0), source 'P', suffix, castle, ep, result.
  Newboard: `\1` + 'P' (E) + `\3` + '.'(captured removed) + `\5` + '.'(source cleared) + `\7`.
  Wait after target E, between `\3` (7 squares) includes squares E+1..E+7, but the captured pawn at E+8 is removed, so that group `\3` includes the square E+7 which is... let's layout: E at position, then 7 chars (positions E+1..E+7), then captured 'p' at E+8, then 0 between, then source P at E+9. So newboard: `\1` + 'P' + `\3` + '.' + `\5` + '.' + `\7`. Here `\3` spans 7 chars and includes the square E+7 (which is the "behind" square? Actually after removing captured pawn, the captured square E+8 becomes '.', and source E+9 becomes '.'; the squares between remain unchanged). Good.

Also need EP field to match the protected token (e.g., `eh` for e6). We'll build literal token from file letter + placeholder for rank 6.

Additionally, if en passant capture would put the king in check, general filter removes it. Also en passant doesn't affect castle rights (generic update later).

Now, what about en passant with EP field '-'? No match.

One subtlety: In the input FEN, the EP field indicates the en passant target square, but only if the last move was a double pawn push. If the EP token is present but no black pawn actually on the fifth rank adjacent, the move is not legal; our pattern requiring 'p' at E+8 and white pawn at E±1 handles.

Another subtlety: There is a rule that en passant is only legal if the capturing pawn is on the fifth rank. Our S=E±1 with E rank6 (row2) yields S row3 (rank5), correct.

#### Castling generation

Let's implement the two castling moves as rules.

Let's define helper `attacked_prefix_or_pattern(q, require_king=False)`.

We'll build a regex fragment that matches if square q is attacked (prefix pattern). For castle rule, we embed `(?!(?:frag1|frag2|...))` for each q we need.

But `(?!)` inside a larger pattern at position 0: we need the fragments to be anchored with `^`? Since lookahead is at position 0 of the string, a fragment like `^.{S}p.{d}` will only match if the configuration starts at position 0 (which it does since board starts at 0). Good.

Let's write `attack_fragment(q)` returning a regex string (without `^` maybe with `^`) that matches from start if square q is attacked by any black piece advent.

We'll generate:
- For each black pawn source S that attacks q (S = q-7 or q-9, valid row/file): fragment `^.{S}p.{d}` where d=q-S-1.
- For each black knight source S at knight offset: `^.{S}n.{d}`.
- For each black bishop/queen diagonal source S on diagonal: path clear => `^.{S}[bq](\.{d})`.
- For each black rook/queen orthogonal source S: `^.{S}[rq](\.{d})`.
- For black king source S adjacent: `^.{S}k.{d}`.

Where for S<q. For S>q (attacker after target), fragments:
- Pawn: can a black pawn attack a square "above" it? Black pawn moves downward (increasing row), so it attacks squares with larger index. Thus a black pawn always attacks q where q > SUSTAIN. So only S<q for pawn? Wait if black pawn at S and attacks q diagonally forward (downward, toward white), q index > S. Since board row increases with index; black pawn at rank7 (row1) attacks rank6 (row2) which has larger index. So S<q. For white king on row3 (rank5), black pawns below (higher index) attack upward? Black pawns move from rank7 to rank1, i.e., increasing indexches. So no black pawn attacks a square "above" it. Correct, pawn fragments only S<q.
- Knight: symmetric, both S<q and S>q.
- Bishop/rook/queen: both directions.
- King: adjacent both.

For S>q:
- Leapers (knight/king): `^.{q}.[black_piece].{S-q-1}`? Pattern: `^.{q}.{d}[black_piece]` where d=S-q-1; this ensures the black piece at S.
- Sliders: `^.{q}(\.{d})[black_piece]`.

Now for check detection (king at q), we need require 'K' at q. We can create attack_check_fragment(q) similar but with K at position q:
- S<q: sliders `^.{S}[bqr](\.{d})K`; leapers `^.{S}[pnk].{d}K` (but for pawn/king/knight). Actually for pawn and knight and king, no path, so `^.{S}[pnk].{d}K`.
- S>q: sliders `^.{q}K(\.{d})[bqr]`; leapers `^.{q}K.{d}[pnk]`.

For castle transit (empty square), use general fragments.

Now, build castle rule:

White kingside castle (e1->g1):
- Source s=60 (king), dest t=62 (king), rook from 63 to 61.
- Board conditions in the original board:
  - B[60]=='K' (white king on e1)
  - B[63]=='R' (rook h1)
  - B[61]=='.' and B[62]=='.' (f1,g1 empty)
  - Castle field contains 'K'.
- Attack preconditions: e1 not attacked, f1 not attacked, g1 not attacked.
  e1 contains K, so use check fragment for q=60. f1/g1 empty, use attack fragment q=61 and q=62.
- Move effect: B[60]='.', B[61]='R', B[62]='K', B[63]='.'.
- Rights: original castle (generic update later removes K and Q because king off e1). EP = '-'.

Pattern construction:
We need to match the full working line with board and castle/ep/result, and also embed the negative lookahead for attacks.

Full pattern:
```
^(?!(?:ATTACK_60|ATTACK_61|ATTACK_62))(.{60})K(R? .)...
```

Let's build precisely.

Board part: We want to capture the board so we can reconstruct after castling. Since modifications involve four squares (60,61,62,63), we can split board into segments:
- Group1: first 60 chars (`(.{60})`)
- Then char at 60 must be 'K': literal `K`
- Group2: chars 61-62 (two chars): must be `..` (both empty)
- Group3: char at 63 must be 'R': literal `R`
- Then `|` + castle + `|` + ep + `>` + result.

But we also need to capture castle and ep for replacement. Pattern:
`^(?!(?:...))(.{60})K(\.\.)(R)\|([^|]*)\|([^|]*)>(.*)$`

Wait after board char 63, next char is `|`. So pattern:
`^` + lookahead + `(.{60})K(\.\.)(R)\|([^|]*)\|([^|]*)>(.*)$`

Groups:

6: result

Where newboard = `\1.RK.` (using the same prefix `\1`).

Great.

Thus:
- Kingside lookahead: `(?!F60_check|F61_attack)` (e1 check, f1 attack). Optionally include g1.
- Queenside: `(?!F60_check|F59_attack)` (e1 check, d1 attack). Optionally c1.

Let's include g1/c1 in lookahead too for completeness, no harm.

Let's verify with a simple move, e.g., white pawn e2-e4 (single? double). Starting position white pawn at e2 index? rank2 row6, file4 => 6*8+4=52. Double to e4 rank4 row4 index36? Wait rank4 row4, file4 => 4*8+4=36. So s=52, t=36, s>t. gap = 52-36-1 = 15. path_empty True. piece_cls='P', target_cls='\.'. Target at 36 must be '.', and squares between (37..51) all '.' The move from e2 (52) to e4 (36): squares between indices 37..51 = 15 squares, must be empty. Is that correct? The path for a double pawn move should have e3 (index44) empty and e4 empty. The "between squares" in the linear string include all upper ranks' files; requiring them all to be '.' would be wrong! Because from e2 to e4, only e3 and e4 need to be empty, not the entire 15 linear indices (which include other squares like f4, a3, etc.).

This is a critical issue: The flat 1D representation with regex `.{gap}` is only valid for moves along the same file or same row? Let's reconsider.

In a flat row-major string, moving vertically in the same file corresponds to an offset of ±8, and the squares *between* are exactly the 7 squares before/after in the flat string? Wait if target is directly above source (same file), then t = s - 8. The squares "between" them in the linear string are positions t+1 ... s-1, which are 7 chars *on the rank above*, not the same file column! Example: e4 index36, e2 index52. Positions 37..51 are all squares between e4 and e2 in the flat string, but they are NOT the squares between e4 and e2 on the chessboard. The actual squares between are e3 (44), which is one of those chars. So requiring all 15 chars to be '.' is far too strict.

Therefore, the flat row-major 1D string is not suitable for simple regex path checking of vertical moves using `.{gap}`, because the intervening chars in the string correspond to a swath of the board, not the path.

Hmm. This is a major design flaw. We need a representation where moves along files are contiguous in the string, or handle path checking differently.

Options:
1. Use a board representation with ranks as separate lines and match vertical moves via explicit `\n` patterns. For example, use an 8-line string (each rank an 8-char line). Then moving up one rank corresponds to same column in previous line. Path checking for a vertical move is naturally "same column in each intermediate line". But regex matching for diagonal/sliding moves across lines is still complex but doable.

2. Use flat board but with a "column-major" or transposed order so that vertical moves are contiguous. If we order squares by file then rank (files as rows), then moving along a file becomes adjacent in the string, allowing path checks with `.{gap}`. But horizontal moves then become non-contiguous (offset 8). We could use a transposed representation where vertical moves are linear; however sliders move in both directions. We need path clearing for both orth和diag.

Maybe use a 2D grid with newlines and regex patterns that can say "same column across multiple lines". For vertical ray, pattern like `R\n.{7}` etc. Hmm.

Alternative: Instead of trying to encode arbitrary moves in flat string, we can use a *per-square* move detector that doesn't rely on `.{gap}` path clearing but instead matches explicit square coordinates via a pattern that computes positions? Regex can't compute.

Let's think about representation more carefully.

We could store the board as 8 lines, each with 8 chars, separated by some delimiter (e.g., `/` or newline). Then:
- Horizontal moves (same rank): positions within a line; moving left/right by 1 is adjacent chars. Path clearing for rook horizontal is easy: e.g., `R\.+` within a line.
- Vertical moves (same file): positions in same column across lines. With lines separated by a delimiter, the pattern from a square to the square above is: current line char, then `.{7}` (rest of line), `\n`, then `.{7}` (start of next line up to same column), target char. Path clearing requires each intermediate line's same column char to be '.'.
- Diagonal moves: require moving across lines and columns simultaneously; pattern more complex but manageable with repeated units.

This is doable but the pattern generation becomes more complexhare. However, we can still generate rules per (s,t) by constructing the exact regex that matches the grid lines with the path squares.

Alternatively, we can keep the flat representation but express path-clearing conditions by requiring *all* squares that lie on the ray to be empty. On the flat string, the ray squares are scattered every 8 positions (for vertical) or every 7/9 (diagonal). We can match them with explicit fixed patterns: e.g., for a vertical move from e2 (52) to e4 (36), the intermediate square is e3 (44). We can write a pattern that checks square 44 is '.' by describing its position: `.{44}\.` etc. For any slide of length k, the intermediate squares are at specific indices; we can construct a regex that includes each intermediate square with the exact prefix-suffix around it. This is possible: For a given move (s,t) with a straight ray, the squares along the ray are s - d, s - 2d, ..., t + d. We can build the pattern to match the board and verify each intermediate square char is '.' by including lookaheads or by capturing the board segments at those exact positions.

But building a pattern that both captures source/target and verifies intermediates can be done by using lookaheads to check each intermediate square, or by capturing the board in segments skipping the intermediates. However, because the ray squares are not contiguous in the flat string, the "middle" between s and t in linear index includes many irrelevant squares; we can't just use `.{}`.

We can use lookaheads anchored at the start: For an intermediate square m, assert `(?=.{m}\.)` (i.e., the char at position m is '.'). We can put all such lookaheads at the beginning of the pattern. Then the main pattern matches source/target positions via explicit `.{s}` etc., without requiring the gap squares to be dots. Since lookaheads check the intermediate squares individually, this verifies path clear.

Great! So we can revise the rule pattern:
- Instead of capturing the full gap with `\.{gap}`, we just need to capture target and piece positions. The "between" group that we used to reconstruct the board (group3) contains all the squares between source and target in the *linear string*, which are not on the ray. But we don't need to alter those squares; they remain unchanged. We only need to capture them as a group to preserve them in the new board. We can capture them as `.{}` (any chars). And separately, use lookaheads to assert the ray's intermediate squares are '.'.

So the generic pattern becomes:
- At the start, include `(?=(?:.{m}\.)*?)`? Actually for each intermediate square m, add lookahead `(?=.{m}\.)` which asserts position m is '.'. Since we're at start of line, `(?=.{m}\.)` checks the m-th char is '.'.
- Then main pattern: for s<t, `^(.{s})(PIECE)(.{t-s-1})(TARGET)(.{63-t})\|...` with the gap group being any chars (we still need to preserve them). It doesn't require them to be dots.
- For s>t similarly.

This means we can keep the flat representation and use lookaheads to check the actual ray squares.

Let's verify with e2-e4: source 52, target 36, intermediate square 44 (e3). Add lookahead `(?=.{44}\.)`. It asserts board[44]=='.'. Target 36 '.' via target group. Good. No need to check 37..43 etc.

For a rook move from a1 (56) to a4 (40?) Let's compute a1 index row7 file0=56, a4 row4 file0=32? Wait row4=32. Actually moving up 4 ranks: 56 -> 48 (a2? row6) -> 40 (a3 row5) -> 32 (a4 row4). Intermediates 48,40. Lookaheads for those squares. Source 32? Wait if source a1=56 and target a4=32, intermediates 48,40. Add lookaheads `(?=.{48}\.)` and `(?=.{40}\.)`. Target 32 must be empty/black (matched by target class). Good.

Thus the flat representation works with lookaheads for path clearance.

Excellent! This solves the issue. Let's redefine pattern construction:

For a move s,t with piece and target, and a set of intermediate ray squares `intermediates` (indices strictly between s and t along the ray), the pattern is:
```
^(?=lookahead_for_each_intermediate)(.{min(s,t)})(target_or_piece... etc)
```
Wait we need to insert lookaheads at start, before the board capture. So pattern starts with `^` then for each m in intermediates: `(?=.{m}\.)` (or `(?=.{m}[.])`). Then the main capturing groups as before but with gap = `.{abs(s-t)-1}` (any chars), not requiring dots.

But note: For a slider move, the target must be not occupied by a white piece; target class `[.]` or `[pnabrq]`. The intermediate squares must be empty; lookaheads enforce.

For leapers (knight/king/pawn), intermediates list is empty (they don't slide).

This removes the need for `path_empty` to affect the gap pattern; gap is always `.{gap}`.

Let's update the generic rule builder accordingly:

```
def add_rule(s, t, piece_cls, target_cls, intermediate_indices, new_piece, ep_lit):
    # intermediate_indices: list of board indices (0..63) between source and target along the move that must be '.'.
    prefix_lookaheads = ''.join(f'(?=.{{{m}}}\\.)' for m in intermediate_indices)
    if s < t:
        pat = '^' + prefix_lookaheads + f'(.{{{s}}})({piece_cls})(.{{{t-s-1}}})({target_cls})(.{{{63-t}}})\\|([^|]*)\\|([^|]*)>(.*)$'
        ...
    else:
        pat = '^' + prefix_lookaheads + f'(.{{{t}}})({target_cls})(.{{{s-t-1}}})({piece_cls})(.{{{63-s}}})\\|([^|]*)\\|([^|]*)>(.*)$'
```

This works because lookaheads are zero-width at start and don't consume; then the main capture proceeds.

Let's test on e2-e4: s=52,t=36 (s>t). prefix lookahead `(?=.{44}\.)`. Pattern s>t:
`^(?=.{44}\.)(.{36})(\.)(.{15})(P)(.{11})\|...`
Groups: 1=first 36 chars, 2=target (must be '.'), 3=gap chars (positions 37..51 any), 4=P at 52, 5=suffix 53..63 (11 chars). Great. The lookahead ensures board[44]=='.'.

Now for a knight move, no intermediates; gap chars between source/target can include anything; target class checked.

For a king move, gap chars maybe include adjacent pieces but path irrelevant; target class checked.

Now en passant rules: intermediates none (diagonal one step); we'll handle separately.

Castling rules don't need path lookaheads (we check empties explicitly).

This is much better!

Let's also consider a rook horizontal move: e.g., rook a1(56) to h1(63), source 56 target 63, intermediates 57..62 (six squares). Add lookaheads for each to be '.'. Pattern s<t with gap=6 any chars; lookaheads ensure those specific positions are '.'. Good.

For diagonal bishop move from c1(58) to f4? Let's compute; the ray squares have specific indices; lookaheads cover them.

So our generic builder can take `intermediate_indices` list.

Now, for pawn double push: intermediate includes only s-8 (the skipped square), not all in-between. Good.

Let's also consider that a sliding piece path may be blocked by any piece (white or black). Our lookaheads require intermediate squares to be '.' which covers both. Correct.

Now for captures: target class `[pnabrq]`; intermediate '.'; fine.

For non-captures: target class `\.`.

### Check attack fragments with flat board

Now the attack detection fragments also need to account for path clearance correctly using lookaheads on specific ray squares, not `\.{gap}` in the middle of the string. For a slider attack on square q from source S, the path consists of squares along the ray between S and q. We need to assert they are '.'; we can add lookaheads in the fragment pattern as well. For an attack fragment matching from start, we can write:

- If S < q:
  - Slider: `^.{S}[black_piece]` + (for each intermediate m between S and q: `(?=.{m}\.)`) + `.{q-S-1}` (any chars) + `K?` 
  But wait after `^.{S}[black_piece]`, we need to ensure the squares between are empty. Since the main consumption continues through the `.{q-S-1}` any chars, we can put lookaheads *before* consuming, e.g., `^.{S}[black_piece](?:(?=.{m}\.) for m)... .{q-S-1}`. But lookaheads can be placed anywhere; `(?=.{m}\.)` checks from start position? Lookahead at current position checks from current position, not from start. If we place `(?=.{m}\.)` at position after consuming `.{S}[black_piece]` (i.e., current position S+1), the lookahead `(?=.{m}\.)` would try to match `.{m}\.` starting at S+1, which would be wrong (it would check position S+1+m). To check absolute position m from the start of string, we need the lookahead at the beginning (position 0): `(?=.{m}\.)`. So for attack fragments, we should put all path lookaheads at the very beginning (after `^`), before consuming the piece prefix.

Thus a fragment for an attack on q by slider at S<q with intermediates list M:
`^(?:(?=.{m}\.) for m in M).{S}[bq].{q-S-1}` (no K if empty square attack; if check detection, then include K at q).

Actually we need to match that the black piece at S attacks q. The fragment should match up to at least the position after the source piece. If we use `^.{S}[bq]` to consume prefix+piece, and then lookaheads are before that consumption but they check absolute indices. Since after `^`, at position 0, lookaheads `(?=.{m}\.)` can be before `.{S}[bq]`. Example: `^(?=.{m}\.){S}[bq].{q-S-1}`. This checks board[m]=='.' for each m, then consumes S chars, black piece, then consumes the rest between. This matches if the ray between is clear (because all intermediate squares are '.'). It doesn't verify q content)Skip (which is fine for attack-on-empty-square).

For check detection (king at q attacked), fragment: `^(?:(?=.{m}\.)...).{S}[bq].{q-S-1}K` — after consuming between, it requires K at q.

Similarly for S>q slider: `^(?:(?=.{m}\.)...).{q}K(?:.{S-q-1})[bq]`? Pattern: consume prefix to q, require K at q, consume between, require black piece at S. With lookaheads at start for intermediate empties. For attack on empty square q: `^(?:(?=...)).{q}.{S-q-1}[bq]` (no K requirement).

For a knight attack (leaper): no path lookaheads. Fragment S<q: `^.{S}[n].{q-S-1}` (and optionally K at q for check). Since the geometry already ensures the knight attacks q if the distance is a knight move. But wait the `.{q-S-1}` can be any chars; the square q is right after. This pattern matches if there's a black knight at S and q is at knight distance. Good.

For black pawn attack: S<q, pattern `^.{S}p.{q-S-1}` (no path). Note the geometry for pawn: q - S is 7 or 9 (diagonal down one rank). `.{q-S-1}` length 6 or 8. Works.

For black king attack: S<q, pattern `^.{S}k.{q-S-1}` etc.

Now, we also need to be careful about boundary: The `.{q-S-1}` between could cross file boundaries? But since we only generate fragments for geometrically valid attacks (same diagonal/orth file etc.), the pattern will only match if the linear distance corresponds to that geometry. Because we enumerate only valid S,q pairs, the intermediate chars being arbitrary is fine; the pattern doesn't need to verify file boundaries because the indices already imply the boundary.

Great.

Now let's revise the attack fragment functions.

`attack_fragment(q)` returns a regex string (to be used inside `(?:...)` or `(?!...)`) that matches from `^` if square q is attacked by any black piece[empty-square attack]. We'll generate all fragments and join with `|`.

For each black piece type, enumerate sources S such that S attacks q:
- Black pawn: as above.
- Black knight.
- Black bishop/queen (diagonal).
- Black rook/queen (orthog).
- Black king.

For each S, path intermediates list (if slider) computed.

Then fragment string:
- If S<q:
  - prefix = `^`
  - for m in intermediates: `(?=.{m}\.)`
  - `.{S}` + piece_class + `.{q-S-1}`
  (If we want to require K at q for check, append `K` after the `.{q-S-1}`.)
- If S>q:
  - prefix = `^`
  - for m in intermediates: `(?=.{m}\.)`
  - `.{q}` + (for check: `K`) + `.{S-q-1}` + piece_class
  (For empty square attack, no `K`.)

Note: for S<q and slider, the intermediate squares are S+1 ... q-1; the consumption `.{S}` + piece + `.{q-S-1}` consumes through just before q. For S>q, consumes prefix through q, then `.{S-q-1}`, then piece at S.

Let's define `intermediate_indices_along_line(s, t)` for sliding moves: for a straight ray with step d, intermediates are s+d, s+2d, ..., t-d.

We'll have helper to compute ray steps.

### Check filter rule

Now the attack fragments for check. For each possible white king square q (where K might be), we create full line patterns that match if the line has a white king at q under attack. But since we don't know q, we can create one big alternation of patterns each anchored at start and consuming through the board, castle, ep. We want to delete lines that contain check.

A check pattern for a specific q and attacker S:
- The full line is `BOARD|CASTLE|EP`. We need to match the board such that K at q and attacked, and also match through the `|CASTLE|EP` to the end so that `(?m)^...$\n?` can delete the entire line. Since our lines don't have newlines initially? After we convert result `@` to newline, each line is `BOARD|CASTLE|EP`. So check removal rule will be `(?m)^(?:CHECK_PATTERN1|CHECK_PATTERN2|...)$\n?` -> ``.

Each CHECK_PATTERN must match from start to end of the line. For a given q and attacker S, pattern:
- If S<q: `^` + lookaheads(path) + `.{S}[black_piece].{q-S-1}K` + `.{63-q}` + `\|[^|]*\|[^|]*` (castle and ep) + `$`.
  Wait after K at q, the rest of board is `.{63-q}` (suffix), then `|castle|ep`. We can just do `.*` to the end: after the K, `.*` would consume rest including `|...`. Since the line ends after ep, `.*$` matches to end. So we can simplify: after the attack pattern (up to K at q), use `.*$` to consume the rest. Similarly for S>q patterns.
  Because we're matching to delete the line, the pattern needs to cover from start to end, so we can append `.*$`.
- So a check fragment = `^` + (path lookaheads) + (attack prefix through K) + `.*$`.

For example S<q slider: `^(?=...).{S}[bq].{q-S-1}K.*$`.

Then the deletion rule: `(?m)^(?:FRAG1|FRAG2|...)$\n?` with empty replacement. If a line has check, one of the fragments matches, so the whole line (including trailing newline) is removed.

But wait: The line contains `BOARD|CASTLE|EP`; the check fragment `^...(K).*$` uses `.*` to consume rest including the `|`. Good.

Now, one subtlety: The deletion rule with `(?m)` and `^...$\n?`: In Python, `$` matches before newline; `\n?` optionally consumes the newline. When multiple lines, `re.sub` will match each illegal line and remove it including newline? Let's test later.

We can also make a simpler deletion: replace `\n` with something? We'll test.
